Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Epictetus' Philosophy••
	• Focuses on the importance of living up to moral standards
	• Treats displays of weakness or failure as shameful
	• Encourages individuals to reflect on their own behavior

â€¢ ••The Relevant Audience••
	• The audience is not external observers, but the individual's own "literary self"
	• The purpose is to confront and ask individuals how they would judge their own behavior

â€¢ ••The Father's Example••
	• A father is distraught by his daughter's sickness and cannot bear to stay at her bedside
	• Epictetus asks if the father is acting from love, and the father responds that he is acting "naturally"
	• Epictetus responds that the father's abandonment is contrary to the nature of love

â€¢ ••The Importance of Autonomy••
	• Epictetus' Stoicism emphasizes autonomy, self-determination, and freedom from external constraint
	• Individuals' actions are meaningful insofar as they are deliberately and freely chosen

â€¢ ••The Meaning of Life••
	• Epictetus sets the bar for a meaningful life high, but the height is relative to individual personality and endowments
	• Only a few have the potential to become suitable subjects for public recognition and eulogistic biography

â€¢ ••Self-Knowledge and Social Role••
	• Epictetus emphasizes the achievement of self-knowledge and excellent performance of social role
	• Individuals should strive to excel in their roles as father, brother, wife, soldier, magistrate, etc.

â€¢ ••The Value of Everyday Life••
	• Epictetus' philosophy is compatible with the idea that there is sufficient meaning in the human world
	• He endorses the need for an account of life's meaning to distinguish between the animal self and the rational self

â€¢ ••The Role of Achievement••
	• Epictetus lays great stress on achievement, but what he urges his students to achieve is not fulfillment of a specific life plan
	• He emphasizes the importance of individual actions and deliberate choice

â€¢ ••The Limitations of Grandiose Notions••
	• Epictetus would be less sympathetic to notions that the meaning of life requires a specific life plan or grandiose decision
	• He emphasizes the importance of attention to day-to-day existence and the episodic nature of life

â€¢ ••The Significance of Epictetus' Philosophy••
	• Epictetus' philosophy remains relevant for modern investigation of the meaning of life
	• His emphasis on autonomy, self-determination, and the importance of everyday life continues to inspire and guide individuals.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Sextus Empiricus' Skepticism••
	• A late 2nd-century Pyrrhonist who claimed no knowledge or beliefs
	• Would not assert anything about the meaning of life or its existence

â€¢ ••The Goal of Skeptics••
	• To achieve tranquility or the absence of anxiety (ataraxia)
	• Not necessarily a more profound goal, but rather a state of being

â€¢ ••The Skeptical Process••
	• Involves suspending belief in the face of conflicting appearances and accounts
	• Uses methods such as "modes" (e.g. Aenesidemus and Agrippa) to achieve this suspension

â€¢ ••The Basis of Skeptical Actions••
	• Without beliefs, skeptics must rely on appearances and accounts
	• Must be willing to revise their views in the face of new evidence

â€¢ ••The Achievement of Tranquility••
	• Skeptics become tranquil by suspending belief and living in the absence of anxiety
	• This is not necessarily a result of reaching the truth, but rather a state of being

â€¢ ••David Hume's Critique••
	• Hume suggested that the anxiety skeptics sought to avoid is a more likely result of their skepticism
	• This critique challenges the idea that skeptics can achieve tranquility through their methods

â€¢ ••The Nature of Anxiety••
	• Initial intellectual anxiety prompts investigations into the nature of things
	• This anxiety is characteristic of intellectual talent, but not universal

â€¢ ••The Impasse of Skeptical Inquiry••
	• Skeptics are compelled to withhold assent to any claim about reality
	• This impasse is a result of the equal persuasiveness of conflicting appearances and accounts

â€¢ ••The Skeptical Experience••
	• Skeptics' experience is different from what one might expect
	• They do not become troubled by the impasse, but rather achieve tranquility

â€¢ ••The Legacy of Sextus Empiricus••
	• His skeptical arguments have retained interest through the ages
	• However, his account of tranquility has not impressed many, including David Hume

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The goal of skepticism••
	• The goal of skepticism is to attain tranquility in matters of belief
	• This tranquility is accompanied by moderation of feeling (metriopatheia)

â€¢ ••Suspension of belief••
	• Suspension of belief is a result of scrupulous investigations
	• It is a necessary condition for achieving tranquility

â€¢ ••Investigations••
	• Investigations are conducted in earnest to secure tranquility
	• The purpose of investigations is not to establish truth or falsity, but to suspend belief

â€¢ ••Fair and rigorous investigation••
	• Sceptics must investigate accounts with fairness and rigor
	• They must approach each account with an open mind, without bias

â€¢ ••Hypercritical approach••
	• Sceptics must be hypercritical and armed with arguments to upset any positive or negative account of reality
	• This approach is necessary to maintain tranquility

â€¢ ••Distinguishing scepticism from dogmatism••
	• Scepticism is distinct from dogmatism, which involves entertaining beliefs
	• Sceptics do not hope to find truth, but rather tranquility in matters of belief

â€¢ ••The problem of anxiety••
	• Having beliefs entails anxiety, according to the sceptics
	• This anxiety is a genuine problem that sceptics aim to overcome

â€¢ ••Living without commitment••
	• Sceptics live by their appearances, without commitment to their truth or falsity
	• Appearances are beliefs without commitment, or "without strong inclination or adherence"

â€¢ ••The nature of appearances••
	• Appearances are what appear to be the case, without any commitment to their truth or falsity
	• They are a way of living without beliefs, but still engaging with the world

â€¢ ••The sceptic's disposition••
	• The sceptic's disposition is characterized by a willingness to suspend belief and live with appearances
	• This disposition is distinct from that of dogmatists, who entertain beliefs and hope to find truth.

Here are 10 bullets summarizing the text with sub-bullets:

â€¢ ••Sextus' Account of Sceptical Appearances••
	• Appearances can be contradictory and uncertain
	• Anything can appear to be the case, regardless of reality

â€¢ ••Juxtaposing Appearances••
	• Sextus juxtaposes appearances of different observers and thinkers
	• He finds that one appearance can be matched with a contrary appearance

â€¢ ••Scrutinizing Accounts••
	• Sextus scrutinizes the accounts given of these appearances
	• He finds no reason to accept one appearance as true over another

â€¢ ••Suspension of Belief••
	• Sextus suspends belief about which appearance is true
	• He does not give priority to what appears to be the case to the majority

â€¢ ••Equipollence••
	• Sextus explains suspension of belief as a result of equipollence
	• Equipollence is equality with regard to being persuasive and unpersuasive

â€¢ ••Persuasion and Suspension of Belief••
	• The sceptic may be persuaded by one account, but still suspend belief
	• The sceptic may retain or change their mind, but still suspend belief

â€¢ ••The Sceptic's Attitude••
	• The sceptic has no means to decide which account to be persuaded by
	• The sceptic is compelled to suspend belief due to the opposed accounts

â€¢ ••The Role of Argumentative Insights••
	• The sceptic's argumentative insights and skills play a role in suspension of belief
	• The sceptic asks themselves: "ought I to be persuaded by what persuades me?"

â€¢ ••The Sceptic's Experience••
	• The sceptic may experience appearances that contradict each other
	• The sceptic may be persuaded by one appearance, but still suspend belief

â€¢ ••The Nature of Scepticism••
	• Scepticism is not about being equally persuaded by contradictory appearances
	• Scepticism is about being unable to decide which account to be persuaded by

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Sextus' skepticism••
	• Sextus suspends belief due to the equal force of opposing accounts
	• He cannot adjudicate between different but equally authoritative accounts
	• This suspension of belief is a passive reaction to equal persuasiveness

â€¢ ••Appearance vs. Belief••
	• Appearance is a passive experience, whereas belief involves active assent
	• The sceptic does not willingly pass judgment on the truth of an appearance
	• Suspension of belief is an example of a passive reaction to equal persuasiveness

â€¢ ••The nature of appearance••
	• Appearance is characterized as an affection (pathos)
	• It lies in feeling and unwilled affection
	• The sceptic acknowledges that he has specific appearances and not others

â€¢ ••Sources of persuasion••
	• The sceptic can be persuaded by various things, such as:
		- Other people
		- Clever rhetoric
		- Forceful arguments
		- Cultural background
		- Family and friends
		- Personal interests

â€¢ ••Reasoning as a source of persuasion••
	• Sextus never rejects reasoning as the source of the sceptic's appearances
	• The sceptic can engage in thought that arises from arguments, but does not imply reality

â€¢ ••The sceptic's actions••
	• The sceptic conducts his life by following observation (te-re-sis) of life
	• This is a term of art borrowed from the Empiricist school of medicine
	• The sceptic's criterion is similar to the Empiricist medical practice

â€¢ ••The sceptic's passivity••
	• The sceptic is portrayed as a passive reactor to external stimuli
	• He imitates what he takes to be ordinary life, without much reflection
	• This has been criticized as a shallow and unreflective approach to life

â€¢ ••The sceptic's acknowledgment of appearances••
	• The sceptic acknowledges that he has specific appearances and not others
	• He can explain why he has particular appearances and not others
	• He can point to some argument or reason for his appearances

â€¢ ••The sceptic's suspension of belief••
	• The sceptic suspends belief due to the equal force of opposing accounts
	• This suspension of belief is a passive reaction to equal persuasiveness
	• It does not imply a complete lack of knowledge or understanding

â€¢ ••The sceptic's relationship to reality••
	• The sceptic does not imply the reality of things thought
	• He engages in thought that arises from arguments, but does not assume reality
	• His skepticism is focused on the limits of knowledge, rather than the nature of reality.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Distinction between evident and non-evident••
	• Sextus Empiricus introduces a distinction between the evident (immediately apprehensible) and the non-evident (inferentially apprehensible)
	• This distinction is later shown to be misleading

â€¢ ••Pyrrhonist practical criterion••
	• The Pyrrhonist acts only on the basis of what they passively observe without reflection
	• This is not the same as the sceptic's actual criterion of action

â€¢ ••Contrast between apparent and real••
	• Sextus relies on a contrast between how things appear and how they really are
	• The sceptic suspends belief about the non-evident, as they make no claims about how things really are

â€¢ ••Observation of life••
	• Sextus identifies four aspects of observation of life:
		- Perceiving and thinking
		- Necessitation of affections (e.g. thirst leading to drinking)
		- Following laws and customs
		- Teaching of expertise

â€¢ ••Perceiving and thinking••
	• The Pyrrhonist has typical human qualities, including the capacity to reflect
	• However, Sextus does not emphasize critical reflection as a capability

â€¢ ••Necessitation of affections••
	• Examples include thirst leading to drinking and hunger to eating
	• This aspect of observation is not necessarily reflective or critical

â€¢ ••Following laws and customs••
	• The sceptic passively acquiesces in the values inculcated in them through customs and laws
	• This is not necessarily a critical or reflective process

â€¢ ••Teaching of expertise••
	• Observation and experience are sufficient for imparting skills
	• However, Sextus later criticizes the dogmatic tendencies of Empiricism

â€¢ ••Methodist influence••
	• Sextus later breaks with the Empiricists and adopts a Methodist outlook
	• The Methodist notion of the evident is wider and includes inferential reasoning

â€¢ ••Sceptical criterion of action••
	• The sceptical criterion of action is made to harmonize with the Methodist outlook
	• This is achieved through the second item on Sextus' list, necessitation of affections

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Empiricists and Tranquillity••
	• The Empiricists take objects of inferential reasoning to be objects of inferential reasoning.
	• Tranquillity is the result of suspension of belief, not a particular way of life.
• ••Sextus' Explanation••
	• Sextus explains the sceptics' aim at tranquillity through a story about the painter Apelles.
	• Apelles creates a perfect picture of foam by throwing a sponge at the painting.
• ••The Analogy with Medicine••
	• Tranquillity is analogous to health on the Empiricist account.
	• Pyrrhonism is therapeutic like medicine, aiming to heal conceit and rashness of dogmatists.
• ••The Empiricist Explanation••
	• The Empiricist explanation of the origins of treatments is given in terms of chance.
	• Galen's account in An Outline of Empiricism explains that cases of knowledge come about spontaneously by chance or nature.
• ••The Chance Result of Suspending Belief••
	• The advent of tranquillity is the chance result of suspending belief in the face of equipollence.
	• The result has been noted, and if one wants to attain tranquillity, one needs to suspend belief in the appropriately rigorous manner.
• ••The Talents of a Sceptic••
	• Attaining tranquillity requires the talents of a sceptic.
	• Sceptics need to be forced by equipollence to suspend belief, not by deciding to suspend belief.
• ••The Outlines of Pyrrhonism••
	• The preserved works of Sextus are the Outlines of Pyrrhonism and Against the Dogmatists.
	• The Outlines of Pyrrhonism is a general introduction to Sextus' Pyrrhonism.
• ••Aenesidemus and Pyrrhonism••
	• Aenesidemus was a philosopher who revived the ideas of Pyrrho of Elis.
	• Aenesidemus broke with the sceptical Academy of the day.
• ••Avicenna and the Meaning of Life••
	• The chapter addresses the meaning of life through a neo-Avicennist perspective.
	• The exercise-in-thinking reflects on the entailments of meditations on the question of being by Avicenna.
• ••Directives••
	• The chapter is not exegetical per se, nor is it aimed at being part of original scholarship.
	• The purpose of the exercise-in-thinking is to reï¬‚ect on the entailments of meditations on the question of being by Avicenna.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Introduction to the Study••
	• The author aims to recover the speech acts of Avicenna, a pre-modern thinker, and disclose their latent intentions.
	• The study focuses on the meaning of life as a theme of inquiry in contemporary philosophy.

â€¢ ••Context of the Study••
	• The author's perspective is set in the context of theorizing about the meaning of life as a theme of inquiry in contemporary philosophy.
	• The study is a continuation of the author's previous work on the question of consciousness.

â€¢ ••Aim of the Study••
	• The aim is to recover the speech acts of Avicenna and disclose their latent intentions without prolepsis, parochialism, or anachronism.

â€¢ ••Avicenna's Philosophical Oeuvres••
	• Avicenna did not directly reflect on the meaning of life.
	• Any analysis of Avicenna's philosophical oeuvres in this context must be mediated via leitmotifs from his thought.

â€¢ ••Conceptual Re-appropriation and Extension••
	• A conceptual re-appropriation and extension of Avicennian concepts within a neo-Avicennist perspective leads to reconsidering Avicenna's ontological reflections on the question of being.

â€¢ ••Ontological Reflections on Being••
	• Avicenna's thinking about the question of being is primarily undertaken in terms of his meditations on the modalities of necessity, contingency-possibility, and impossibility.

â€¢ ••Modalities of Being••
	• The question of the meaning of life is closely related to the question of what it is "to be" rather than "not to be".
	• Avicenna argued that the impossible being cannot exist and that the affirmation of its existence entails a contradiction.

â€¢ ••Impossible Being••
	• The impossible being is what necessarily does not exist, logically or experientially.
	• Examples of impossible beings include a squared-circle and a unicorn.

â€¢ ••Contingent Qua Possible Being••
	• The contingent qua possible being is that whose existence or non-existence is neither impossible nor necessary.
	• It is ontologically neutral and does not entail a contradiction when affirming or negating its existence.

â€¢ ••Connection and Distinction between Mind and Body••
	• Avicenna's thinking on the connection and distinction between mind and body will be considered in relation to his contemplation of the question of being.

â€¢ ••Consequences for the Meaning of Life••
	• The consequences of Avicenna's thinking on the connection and distinction between mind and body will be brought to bear upon the meaning of life.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Existence vs. Essence••
	• Existence is distinct from essence
	• Being is derived from a source other than itself
	• Maintained in being or annihilated by something other than itself

â€¢ ••Necessary Being••
	• Exists in such a way that it essentially cannot but exist
	• Affirmation of its non-existence entails a contradiction
	• Can be due-to-itself or due-to-something-other-than-itself

â€¢ ••Necessary Being due to Itself••
	• Beyond Aristotelian categories
	• Without definition or description
	• Pure Existence without determinateness

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Necessary Being due to Itself••
	• Transcendental character of the question of being
	• Thought by way of concepts without becoming immanent
	• One and only, impossible for it not to be

â€¢ ••Necessary Being due to Something Other than Itself••
	• Necessarily exists in actuality
	• Exists owing to a causal source external to its essence
	• Contingent-being-in-itself brought from potentiality to actuality

â€¢ ••Ontological Difference••
	• Between Being qua Necessary-Being-due-to-Itself and a being qua a-necessary-being-due-to-something-other-than-itself
	• Avicenna's analysis of the ontological modalities of being

â€¢ ••Essence, Quiddity, and Existence••
	• Connection and distinction between essence, quiddity, and existence
	• Resonates with Aristotelian and Kantian concepts

â€¢ ••Aristotelian Categories and Ousiology••
	• Refer back to 'ousia' (substance, subject, essence)
	• Carry resonances with Kantian thesis on being as copula of a judgment

â€¢ ••Avicenna's Necessary Being••
	• Overcomes Aristotelian essentialist ousia-based substance metaphysics
	• Offers a new understanding of necessary being and its characteristics

â€¢ ••Implications of Avicenna's Analysis••
	• Challenges traditional notions of existence and essence
	• Offers a new perspective on the nature of being and its modalities

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Avicenna's Thought Experiment: The "Flying Person"••
	• A thought experiment where a person is generated as a fully grown adult with no sensory perceptions.
	• The person can still affirm the existence of their soul independently of bodily sensation or corporeality.

â€¢ ••The Soul's Existence••
	• The soul is seen as the "first entelechy of a natural body with organs".
	• The soul is non-physical and affected by the physical world.

â€¢ ••Ontological Monism and Dualism••
	• Avicenna's philosophy is characterized by an antinomian ontological monism.
	• This is coupled with an ontic substance dualism, where the mental and corporeal are mutually exclusive.

â€¢ ••The Individuation of the Soul••
	• The soul is individuated by physical laws in the life-world.
	• Consciousness is not explained on the basis of physiology.

â€¢ ••The Soul's Intentional Directionality••
	• The soul has intentional directionality towards what is other than itself.
	• This is achieved through lived situational experiences and the meanings they project in embodied life.

â€¢ ••The Occurrence of Thinking••
	• The occurrence of thinking is a tautological affirmation of "there is [being]" rather than "there is not".
	• This is connected to the idea that the soul is granted being from what is other than itself.

â€¢ ••Empathy and Intersubjective Experience••
	• The soul is connected to others through empathy, analogy, and expectations of reciprocity.
	• This raises the question of how others may experience their life-world.

â€¢ ••Meaning and Otherness••
	• Meaning is not reducible to a Cartesian "I think therefore I am".
	• Meaning is given from otherness, and is not a windowless monad.

â€¢ ••Immortality and the Soul••
	• The Avicennian rational soul is immortal and survives bodily death.
	• The soul retains the form of the proprioception of the sensory-motor composite.

â€¢ ••Eschatology and the Soul's Fate••
	• The soul's fate after death is a mystery.
	• The soul's existence is connected to the idea of being-towards-death and the mysterious future as Otherness.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Soul and the Body••
	• The soul is a rational entity that accompanies the body with its kinetics and mnemonics.
	• The soul is affected by situational experiences, memories, and imaginings that condition its phantom-bodily-self-image.

â€¢ ••Embodiment and Mortality••
	• The body dies when the soul departs from it, freeing the soul for its other-worldly destiny.
	• Corporeal being is not moved by itself, but through a substance other than itself (i.e., the soul).

â€¢ ••The Ontological Question of Being••
	• Avicenna's metaphysics reflects on ontic situational lived experiences in the flesh.
	• The soul is individuated by inner-worldly-embodiment, but separated from the physical body in corporeal death.

â€¢ ••Eschatological Imagining••
	• The soul's eschatological imagining of the afterlife can occasion a promise of hope.
	• The soul is readied for being-towards-the-afterlife in its embodied psychosomatic life.

â€¢ ••Human Existence••
	• Humans are bodily-mortal-existents who are thrown into existence in being-towards-corporeal-death.
	• Embodied living is a borrowed existence granted by what gives being to an indebted bodily-mortal existent.

â€¢ ••The Soul's Immortality••
	• The human soul that survives bodily death is granted its being by what is other than itself.
	• The soul's imagined immortality is marked by what happens in its individuating embodied life.

â€¢ ••The Body and the Soul••
	• The body facilitates or hinders the soul's ability to be in communion with the logos or intellect.
	• The soul is moulded by an embodied life, and this character is retained in the destiny of an immortal disembodied soul.

â€¢ ••Self-Consciousness and Temporal Continuity••
	• The rational soul exists necessarily through what is other than itself.
	• There is temporal continuity in the non-reflection of self-awareness and its immediacy.

â€¢ ••The Soul's Identity••
	• A soul comes into a body and gives it its identity.
	• The body facilitates or hinders the soul's ability to be optimally in communion with the logos or intellect.

â€¢ ••The Soul's Destiny••
	• A non-physical trace of the form of the past body is retained in the soul's self-consciousness.
	• The soul's destiny is shaped by its embodied life and its orientation towards its source.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Past, Present, and Future••
	• The past and future become co-entangled with presence through self-awareness and protention.
	• This relationship is rooted in Avicenna's philosophy (1000-1037 AD).

â€¢ ••Onto-Theology and Divinity••
	• Being is given to existents as temporal borrowed existence, subject to corruption.
	• Thinking can be a mode of thanking what-gives-being, including in the afterlife.

â€¢ ••The Indeterminacy of Necessary Being••
	• Necessary Being is the mystery of what-gives-being, which is indeterminate.
	• This indeterminacy is sublated into a determinateness in being, which is an existent.

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Being and Otherness••
	• Every existent is a necessary-being-due-to-something-other-than-itself.
	• The totality of existents is necessary-due-to-what-is-other-than-itself.

â€¢ ••Emanation and the Chain of Being••
	• The One emanates otherness from itself, whether deterministically or by willing it.
	• This emanation is followed by a return or ascent of the emanated back to the source.

â€¢ ••The Human Being and the Ascent of the Soul••
	• The human being is placed in-between the worldly realm of the flesh and the transcendent domain of spirit.
	• The soul's ascent towards the higher realms is undertaken by its intellective faculties.

â€¢ ••The Return to the Source••
	• The soul is released into actualizing its return to its source after bodily death.
	• This return is the Avicennian account of the mabda' (initiation) and the ma'ad (return).

â€¢ ••Hermeneutical and Exegetical Resonances••
	• The picture of the return to the source has hermeneutical and exegetical resonances with religious scripture.
	• The scripture "We are God's and to Him we shall return" (Qur'an, 2:156) is an example.

â€¢ ••The Meaning of Embodied Life••
	• The meaning of embodied life can be onto-theologically grasped as being-towards-what-grants-being.
	• This being-towards-what-grants-being is within inner-worldly being-towards-corporeal-death.

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Thought and Being••
	• Reflection upon bodily demise can open up to the mystery of other-worldliness as a future released into pure thought.
	• Thinking is the mode through which the question of being is attended to within a mortal's lived experiences.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Meaning of Life is Disclosed through Otherness••
	• The meaning of life is revealed through pondering what grants being via otherness.
	• This involves thinking about what-self-grants-being in the movement of expulsion and what-self-withdraws-being in the movement of attraction.

â€¢ ••Meaning Generation is a Fundamental Aspect of Human Existence••
	• Meaning generation is a fundamental aspect of human existence, even if the locus of such generation is lacking in meaning.
	• Otherness remains at work within selfhood, generating meaning in being-with-others.

â€¢ ••Even in Isolation, We are Still Influenced by Otherness••
	• Even in seeking isolation and self-sufficiency, we are still influenced by otherness.
	• We inherit ways of doing, thinking, acting, and making that are not our own but have been handed down.

â€¢ ••Privacy is Already Indebted to Otherness••
	• Privacy, even at the level of the "flying person", is already indebted to otherness.
	• We are still influenced by social norms, state laws, and other external factors.

â€¢ ••Scientific Research Remains Meaningful••
	• Even if modern science discloses the structure of the material universe, scientific research remains meaningful.
	• The activity of disclosing the principles of matter remains meaningful.

â€¢ ••Our Life-World Provides Pointers for Meaning Generation••
	• Our life-world in being-with-others provides pointers for how we give meaning to our life.
	• Everyday busyness and engagement with others, such as falling in love, parenting, and working with others, provide meaning.

â€¢ ••Meaning Can be Found in Various Ways••
	• Meaning can be found in the present moment, in the past, or in the future.
	• We can give meaning to our living through an onto-theological turn, or by projecting our momentum as a future horizon of inner-worldly possibilities.

â€¢ ••Believing Life is Meaningless Requires an Argument••
	• To state or believe that life is meaningless requires an argument.
	• This argument is itself a mode of generating reasoned meanings through argumentation.

â€¢ ••Meaninglessness Can be Oddly Meaningful••
	• Meaninglessness can become oddly meaningful in the sense that it affects mood, attitude, comportment, and choices.
	• Even in seeking hedonistic lifestyles, we are still influenced by other people and require communicative relations.

â€¢ ••The Nihilist is Forced to Affirm Meaning in Action••
	• The nihilist is forced to affirm meaning in action by way of being-with-others.
	• This can be undertaken in order to have the luxury of refuting meaning in argumentation, or by seeking a lifestyle of bodily pleasures.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Mystery of Being••
	• The question of being is a fundamental mystery that calls for thinking about our own being-in-the-world.
	• This mystery is shaped by technology and our modes of thinking, longing, saying, and doing.
• ••The Impact of Technology••
	• Technology shapes our expectations and actions in everyday life.
	• We are driven by commodity-fetishisms and technicity, which can lead to a loss of meaning and purpose.
• ••The Importance of Meditation••
	• Thinking about being opens up a liminal leeway within the network of technicity.
	• Meditation on the mystery of being can help us find meaning and purpose in life.
• ••The Role of Responsibility••
	• Responsibilities are placed upon us in quotidian dealings.
	• We assume these responsibilities wilfully, or they surge from a call of conscience to sacrifice for others.
• ••The Life and Death Instincts••
	• The life and death instincts of felicity and ruin are signifiers of our engagement with the will of others.
	• We project our will on others, and they impinge upon us, shaping our experiences.
• ••The Attraction to the Source••
	• The meaning of life is underpinned by the grand picture of an attraction of the soul back to its source.
	• This attraction is marked by intimate, inexplicable longings.
• ••The Pathways to Ascent••
	• The pathways for such an ascent will be labyrinthine and tortuous.
	• However, the intellect can guide meditations in this ontic life to reveal how best to return to the One.
• ••Avicenna's Perspective••
	• Avicenna's perspective on the mystical love for the source is that it is best granted its onto-theological meaning via the intellect.
	• The intellect guides meditations to reveal how best to return to the One in the thereafter.
• ••Maimonides and the Meaning of Life••
	• Maimonides was a medieval Jewish philosopher and the author of The Guide of the Perplexed.
	• In this work, Maimonides presents an image of God and His relation to the world that is diametrically opposed to traditional views.
• ••The Significance of Maimonides••
	• Maimonides is widely regarded as the foremost and most famous of medieval Jewish philosophers.
	• He remains the pre-eminent rabbi of all times, at least from the Middle Ages to today, for all who value the Talmudic interpretation of biblical law.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Maimonides' Works••
	• Wrote rabbinic and philosophic compositions
	• Both genres attest to his search for meaning in life
	• Meaning consists in understanding God's nature and living accordingly

â€¢ ••Rabbinic Stature••
	• Based on two legal compendia: commentary on the Mishnah and Mishneh Torah
	• Mishneh Torah is a codification of Talmudic law, organized topically and decisively

â€¢ ••Impact of Mishneh Torah••
	• Threatened the traditional study of the Talmud
	• Posed a threat to the unique form of religiosity cultivated by rabbinical Judaism
	• Sanctioned as the Oral Law, the Talmud was treated as the ongoing connection to the Divine

â€¢ ••Talmud Study••
	• Was a spiritual and religious act that brought a person near to God's presence
	• Was not just an intellectual or legalistic exercise
	• Continued to be studied in its traditional form despite Maimonides' work

â€¢ ••Mishneh Torah's Purpose••
	• Intended to facilitate correct observance of the law
	• Implicitly deprecated further intellectual preoccupation with the law

â€¢ ••Maimonides' Attitude••
	• Strict in his demand that the law be followed
	• Orthodox in his beliefs, but accommodating of Western science

â€¢ ••Influence of Greek Culture••
	• Maimonides wanted to make room for Western science in Judaism
	• Greek culture had clashed with nascent rabbinic Judaism since late classical times

â€¢ ••The Talmud as a Barrier••
	• Served as a wall to protect Jews from persecution and segregation
	• Segregated them from the literary and scientific achievements of the Greco-Roman world

â€¢ ••Maimonides' Legacy••
	• Achieved great renown among his co-religionists
	• His codification of the laws of the Talmud posed a threat to traditional study

â€¢ ••Scholarly Debate••
	• Scholars differ on whether Maimonides' rabbinic or philosophical work best expresses his personal choice for the meaning of life

â€¢ ••Continued Study of the Talmud••
	• Study of the Talmud in its traditional form continues to this day among orthodox Jews

	• He helped to promote a more rational and scientific approach to Jewish thought
	• His writings had a significant impact on Jewish philosophy and theology

	• He wrote this book to enlighten a student on the conflict between Occasionalist beliefs and scientific philosophy
	• The book presents a comprehensive view of Maimonides' philosophical and scientific views

	• He played a significant role in promoting science and rationalism in Jewish thought
	• His writings continue to influence Jewish philosophy and theology to this day

â€¢ ••Aquinas' view of life's meaning••

â€¢ ••Aquinas' view of God's role in life's meaning••
	• Involved God in his philosophy, but not exclusively, and not in the way modern readers tend to suppose

â€¢ ••Aquinas' commitment to Aristotelian essentialism and teleology••
	• Believed that every natural object has an essence or nature, and that this essence grounds teleological features
	• Thought that natural objects are directed towards the realization of certain distinctive ends or goals

â€¢ ••Aquinas' view of natural ends••
	• Believed that natural objects, such as oak trees, are directed towards the realization of certain distinctive ends or goals, such as sinking roots, taking in water, and producing acorns

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Natural objects vs. artifacts••
	• Natural objects have intrinsic teleology (built-in purpose)
	• Artifacts (e.g. watches, beds) have extrinsic or observer-relative teleology (purpose determined by human interests)

â€¢ ••Characteristics of natural objects••
	• Have natural tendencies (e.g. resisting pressure, falling towards the ground)
	• Have an objective standard of goodness and badness (based on realizing their natural ends)

â€¢ ••Aristotelian view of goodness and badness••
	• A thing is good to the extent it realizes its natural ends
	• A thing is bad to the extent it fails to realize its natural ends

â€¢ ••Moral goodness and badness••
	• A special case of the more general kinds of goodness and badness
	• Relates to the goodness or badness of rational beings (who can choose to pursue their natural ends)

â€¢ ••Distinction between natural objects and artifacts••
	• Natural objects have an objective, mind-independent fact of the matter about their goodness and badness
	• Artifacts do not have an objective, mind-independent fact of the matter about their goodness and badness

â€¢ ••God's relationship to the world••
	• Not like a craftsman's relationship to his artifacts (as in William Paley's design argument)
	• God sustains natural objects in being, but they have their own natural ends and purposes

â€¢ ••The point of being a natural object••
	• To realize the ends toward which it is directed by virtue of its nature
	• For example, the point of being an oak is to do oak-like things (e.g. sink roots, produce acorns)

â€¢ ••Human beings as natural objects••
	• Have an essence or nature that determines their natural ends and purposes
	• The point of being a human being is to realize the ends toward which human nature is directed

â€¢ ••Teleology in natural objects••
	• Intrinsic teleology (built-in purpose) is a key feature of natural objects
	• This teleology is not observer-relative, but rather an objective feature of the object itself

â€¢ ••Implications for understanding the world••
	• Natural objects have a built-in purpose and direction
	• This understanding of natural objects is distinct from our understanding of artifacts and human beings.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Human Nature••
	• According to Aquinas and Aristotle, humans are rational animals
	• Our nature includes animal ends (e.g. food, shelter, reproduction)
	• Our distinctive ends are those of the intellect and will (true and good)

â€¢ ••Hierarchical Ordering of Ends••
	• Not all ends are equally significant
	• Lower ends exist for the sake of realizing higher ones
	• Animal ends exist for the sake of realizing ends entailed by rationality

â€¢ ••What Fulfills Us as Humans••
	• Aquinas examines various possible answers
	• Wealth, honor, fame, power, pleasure, and bodily goods are not ultimate goods
	• These things are either means to an end or by-products of a good

â€¢ ••The Soul and Fulfillment••
	• The soul is a created thing and cannot bring perfect fulfillment
	• Only God can bring perfect fulfillment

â€¢ ••Aquinas' Metaphysics••
	• Built on Aristotle's theory of actuality and potentiality
	• Contingent things exist only insofar as their potential is actualized

â€¢ ••The Nature of God••
	• God is the purely actual uncaused cause of things
	• God lacks potentiality and is therefore not caused or needing to be caused

â€¢ ••God as Ultimate Explanation••
	• God is the ultimate explanation of the world
	• Knowing God is necessary for the intellect to fully achieve its natural end

â€¢ ••God as Perfect Goodness••
	• Goodness is realized through actualization
	• God, as pure actuality, is perfectly good

â€¢ ••Being with God••
	• Being with God is necessary for the human will to fully realize its natural end
	• This is because God is the most perfectly good thing

â€¢ ••The Importance of God••
	• God is the foundation of Aquinas' metaphysics and ethics
	• Understanding God is essential for understanding human nature and fulfillment

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God as First Cause and Last End••
	• Aquinas views God as both the source of all things and the ultimate goal of human existence.
	• This theme is present in Plotinus and is central to Aquinas' Summa Theologiae.

â€¢ ••Natural Theology and Law••
	• Aquinas believes that many truths about God and human existence can be known through reason alone.
	• These truths are part of natural theology and natural law, not specific to Christianity.

â€¢ ••Limitations of Natural Capacities••
	• Our natural capacities are limited in their ability to know and understand God.
	• Our intellects and wills are tied to bodies, making it difficult to attain knowledge of God.

â€¢ ••Dangers of Bodily Appetites••
	• Our bodily appetites can distract us from pursuing the highest goods.
	• This can lead to serious moral error and habits of behavior that pull us away from God.

â€¢ ••Incompleteness of Natural Knowledge••
	• Even the most perfect natural knowledge of God is incomplete and abstract.
	• It is easier to grasp what God is not than what he is, and requires analogical language.

â€¢ ••Supernatural Knowledge and Communion••
	• Christianity reveals the possibility of supernatural knowledge of and communion with God.
	• This is not about magic or supernatural powers, but about a deeper understanding of God.

â€¢ ••Supernatural Ends••
	• A supernatural end is one that is above and additional to a thing's natural ends and capacities.
	• Using our natural capacities, our knowledge of and yearning for God is limited and mixed with error.

â€¢ ••Divine Supernatural Assistance••
	• Divine assistance could prevent us from falling into intellectual and moral error.
	• It could also afford us a more intimate knowledge of and communion with God.

â€¢ ••Original Sin and the Loss of Supernatural Gifts••
	• The human race was offered supernatural gifts at creation, but rejected them.
	• The penalty of original sin entails the closing off of the path to attaining the supernatural end of the beatific vision.

â€¢ ••Consequences of Original Sin••
	• The loss of supernatural assistance has led to dysfunction in the operation of our natural intellectual and moral faculties.
	• This has resulted in a state of sin and separation from God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Aristotelian Teleology••
	• Aristotle believed in intrinsic teleology, where objects have a natural purpose
	• This view was later rejected by early modern philosophers like Descartes and Newton

â€¢ ••Early Modern Rejection of Teleology••
	• Early modern thinkers treated all things as artifacts, lacking intrinsic teleology
	• Any teleology was seen as extrinsic, imposed by a divine designer

â€¢ ••Loss of Divine Designer••
	• Later modern philosophers rejected the idea of a divine designer
	• Teleology of even an extrinsic kind was lost, leaving the world without purpose

â€¢ ••Individual Purposes••
	• In the absence of intrinsic teleology, individual human purposes appear arbitrary and contingent
	• These purposes do not reflect anything in the essence or nature of human beings

â€¢ ••Problem of Individual Purposes••
	• The existence of individual purposes is problematic given the modern metaphysical picture
	• These purposes are seen as reducible to patterns of efficient causality or altogether eliminable

â€¢ ••Materialist Explanations••
	• Materialist explanations claim to show that there is no irreducible teleology in human thought and action
	• This leads to the illusion that our purposes and goals are not truly meaningful

â€¢ ••The Problem of the Meaning of Life••
	• Modern philosophers characterize this as the problem of the meaning of life
	• Common sense attributes purposes to our actions and sees purposes in nature, but modern science implies there is no purpose

â€¢ ••Aquinas' View••
	• Aquinas does not frame the question in terms of the meaning of life
	• He believes in a twofold point to human existence: knowledge of God and the beatific vision

â€¢ ••Christian Theology and Teleology••
	• Aquinas incorporates Christian theology into his system of thought
	• He sees human existence as having a supernatural end, the beatific vision, which is possible through divine assistance (grace)

â€¢ ••Recovery of Lost Gifts••
	• Salvation amounts to the recovery of lost supernatural gifts
	• This understanding of Christian theology allows Aquinas to see human existence as having a point and purpose.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Aquinas' perspective on the meaning of life••
	• He assumes that human existence has a meaning or point
	• Unlike modern philosophers, he doesn't doubt the existence of a meaning

â€¢ ••Modern philosophers' perspective on the meaning of life••
	• They doubt that life has a meaning or point
	• They ask "What is the meaning of life?" because they're unsure if there is one

â€¢ ••Aquinas' metaphysical background••
	• He believes in essentialism and teleology
	• Every natural object has a point or natural end

â€¢ ••Implications of Aquinas' metaphysics••
	• Human beings have a natural end or purpose
	• This purpose is inherent in their nature, regardless of God's existence

â€¢ ••Critique of modern philosophers' misgivings••
	• They assume that the point of human existence would be contingent
	• They think that God's purposes would be arbitrary and not inherent in human nature

â€¢ ••Aquinas' response to modern philosophers' concerns••
	• He argues that creation works by inherent natures, not arbitrary stipulations
	• A natural object's purpose is defined by its essence or nature

â€¢ ••The relationship between a thing's nature and its purpose••
	• What is good or bad for a thing is defined by its inherent ends
	• It's metaphysically impossible for a thing to be good for it to act contrary to its ends

â€¢ ••Analogies with other natural objects••
	• An oak tree's purpose is to sink roots, produce acorns, and carry out photosynthesis
	• It wouldn't make sense for the oak tree to pursue a different purpose

â€¢ ••Implications for human existence••
	• Our ends or purposes are simply given to us and could not be otherwise
	• We can't adopt or find use in purposes that are not inherent in our nature

â€¢ ••The question of the meaning of life in Aquinas' metaphysics••
	• The question barely even arises, given his essentialist and teleological metaphysics
	• The meaning of life can be readily given an answer based on human nature

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Question of Meaning••
	• The question "what's it all about?" or "what is the meaning of life?" is a profound and urgent one.
	• It arises in an obvious way, but seems impossible to answer except negatively without a metaphysical background.

â€¢ ••Aristotelian Essentialism and Teleology••
	• Aristotelian essentialism and teleology provide a metaphysical background for answering the question of meaning.
	• See Feser 2014b for detailed exposition and defense of these concepts.

â€¢ ••Aquinas and Paley••
	• Aquinas's views on the existence and nature of God are related to Paley's ideas.
	• See Feser 2013 for a detailed account of this relationship.

â€¢ ••The Relationship Between Science and Metaphysics••
	• Modern science is neutral between an Aristotelian and non-Aristotelian reading.
	• The choice between these readings is ultimately a philosophical question.

â€¢ ••Montaigne's Answer••
	• Montaigne would have answered the question "what's it all about?" with "Work it out for yourself."
	• This answer is an invitation to philosophize and think for oneself.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Individual Inquiry••
	• Since philosophy has not been able to find a way to tranquility that is suitable for all, individuals must seek it out for themselves.
	• Montaigne's answer is not a rejection of philosophy, but rather a call to individual inquiry.

â€¢ ••The Cabbie's Expectation••
	• The cabbie's expectation of an immediate answer is unphilosophical.
	• Montaigne's answer is not a failure to provide an answer, but rather a recognition of the complexity of the question.

â€¢ ••Philosophy as the Love of Wisdom••
	• Philosophy teaches us to think for ourselves and to live.
	• Montaigne's answer is an expression of this idea.

â€¢ ••The Objection to Montaigne's Answer••
	• Some readers may object that Montaigne is concerned with finding meaning in life, rather than the meaning of life.
	• However, Montaigne's answer is actually a response to the question of the meaning of life.

â€¢ ••The Significance of Montaigne's Answer••
	• Montaigne's answer is the best answer that can be given to the question "what's it all about?"
	• It is the only answer that makes sense for a philosopher to give.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Montaigne's Response to Criticism••
	• Montaigne acknowledges two distinct questions posed by critics
	• His answer addresses both questions without implying one is more fundamental
• ••Montaigne's Work••
	• His Complete Works contains over 1,300 pages of essays, letters, and travel journals
	• The work exhibits Montaigne's own thinking and inconsistencies over time
• ••Montaigne's Intentions••
	• He wrote his essays for personal convenience and to preserve his habits and temperament
	• He aimed to portray himself in a simple, natural, and ordinary fashion

	• He warns readers that his essays are not written to seek their favor or glory
	• He presents himself as he truly is, with defects and all
• ••Montaigne's Vanity••
	• He admits to possessing vanity and enjoying the success of his essays
	• However, his note to the reader remains consistent with his character and aims
• ••Montaigne's Aims••
	• He aimed to entertain himself and let his mind idle in full rest
	• He began writing his essays to contemplate the ineptitude and strangeness of his thoughts
• ••The Origins of Montaigne's Essays••
	• They began as a journal of quotations from classical authors
	• Montaigne added his own marginalia and came to realize the project's ambition
• ••Montaigne's View of His Essays••
	• He saw them as an exercise and a ladder for himself, rather than a finished work for readers
	• He believed they were worth more to him than to his readers
• ••Montaigne's Ambition••
	• He realized that an undertaking that began in idleness was actually greatly ambitious
	• He came to see the value in writing down his thoughts and ideas
• ••Montaigne's Legacy••
	• His essays have become a famous and influential work of literature
	• They continue to be studied and appreciated for their insight into Montaigne's character and thinking

â€¢ ••Montaigne's Approach to Knowledge••
	• He was always seeking more knowledge and was not content with what he had learned.
	• He believed that there was always room for a successor and that he himself could continue to learn and grow.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Making Knowledge One's Own••
	• Montaigne believed that simply taking in others' knowledge was not enough.
	• One must make it their own through experience and reflection.

â€¢ ••The Danger of Pedantry••
	• Montaigne warned against being content with simply accumulating knowledge.
	• True wisdom comes from digesting and transforming knowledge into one's own being.

â€¢ ••Montaigne's Writing Habits••
	• He was continually writing on new subjects and adding to his older essays.
	• He was not trying to polish his work, but rather to explore new ideas and insights.

â€¢ ••The Purpose of the Essays••
	• Montaigne's aim was not to provide definitive answers, but rather to explore and learn.
	• He believed that what was useful to him might also be useful to others, but that was not his primary goal.

â€¢ ••Montaigne's Approach to Writing••
	• He strove to move forward and explore new ideas, rather than simply revisiting the same topics.
	• He was fascinated by his own changeability and inconsistencies, and saw his writing as a way to explore himself.

â€¢ ••Recurrent Themes in the Essays••
	• Friendship, human frailty, the variety of custom, and death were all recurring themes.
	• Death was a major preoccupation for Montaigne, and he sought consolation in the face of it.

â€¢ ••Montaigne's View of Death••
	• He believed that death was a natural part of life, and that it was necessary for human flourishing.
	• He saw death as a way to keep humans from becoming too attached to life and to maintain a sense of balance.

â€¢ ••Montaigne's Relationship with Nature••
	• He believed in the importance of living in harmony with nature.
	• He saw nature as a source of wisdom and guidance, and often turned to it for consolation and inspiration.

â€¢ ••Montaigne's Legacy••
	• He is considered the inventor of the essay as a genre.
	• His approach to writing and learning continues to influence thinkers and writers to this day.

â€¢ ••Montaigne's views on death••
	• He sees death as a natural part of life
	• He believes that death is not to be feared, but rather accepted
	• He thinks that people often pity those who are dying unnecessarily

â€¢ ••Montaigne's personal experience with death••

â€¢ ••Montaigne's skepticism about the afterlife••

	• He seems to be more concerned with the present life than with what comes after death

	• He abandoned his earlier stoic views on death and began to see it as a more neutral event

	• He came to see death as simply the end of life, rather than its goal
	• He believed that life should be lived in a way that is consistent with how one wants to die

â€¢ ••Montaigne's views on the nature of consciousness••
	• He believed that consciousness is closely tied to the body
	• He thought that people who are dying are not conscious of their own suffering

â€¢ ••Montaigne's views on the purpose of life••
	• He believed that life should be lived in a way that is consistent with one's values and goals
	• He did not see death as a goal or an end in itself, but rather as a natural part of life

â€¢ ••Montaigne's views on the role of philosophy in life••
	• He believed that philosophy should be used to live life well, rather than to prepare for death
	• He thought that people should focus on living a good life, rather than trying to prepare for the end

â€¢ ••Montaigne's legacy••
	• His essays continue to be studied and admired for their insight into the human condition
	• His views on death and the afterlife remain influential to this day.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Montaigne's perspective on death••
	• He acknowledges the uncertainty of death and the afterlife
	• He cannot declare life to be meaningless, but rather absurd

â€¢ ••The value of difficulty••
	• Montaigne believes that difficulty gives value to things
	• We want adventure, but not too much, or it will lead to disappointment

â€¢ ••Sceptical ambivalence••
	• Montaigne settles for a form of sceptical ambivalence regarding the afterlife
	• He admits that he knows nothing with certainty about the afterlife

â€¢ ••The absurdity of life••
	• Montaigne proclaims the absurdity of life in his essay on vanity
	• We are the investigators without knowledge, the magistrates without jurisdiction

â€¢ ••The fear of nothingness••
	• We fear disappointment at nothingness, even though we may never experience it
	• This fear is rooted in our irrational nature

â€¢ ••The role of comedy and tragedy••
	• Comedy and tragedy are rooted in the difference between what things are and what they ought to be
	• We laugh at disappointments in trivial matters, but weep at disappointments in serious matters

â€¢ ••The nature of laughter and tears••
	• Laughter and tears are a fundamental aspect of human life
	• They are a result of our irrational nature and our desire for things to be different from what they are

â€¢ ••The absurdity of human existence••
	• We are all steeped in absurdity and nonsense, but those who are aware of it are a little better off
	• We cannot get rid of absurdity without getting rid of ourselves

â€¢ ••Appreciating the absurdity of existence••
	• We can come to appreciate the absurdity of our existence, but it does not transcend or reduce its absurdity
	• This appreciation does not make us better off, but rather aware of our absurdity

â€¢ ••The limitations of human knowledge••
	• We are not wholly rational creatures, and our knowledge is limited
	• We can only acknowledge the absurdity of our existence, but not transcend it.

	• Cannot stand on the bottom (i.e., rely on external authorities) nor swim up to the top (i.e., achieve certainty through reason alone)
	• This is not just a rhetorical flourish, but a genuine problem for Descartes

Here are 10 bullets summarizing the text with sub-bullets:

â€¢ ••Descartes' conception of human freedom••
	• Freedom is not a detached power to choose between options
	• Rather, it is the spontaneous assent to what is revealed by "reasons of truth and goodness"

â€¢ ••The role of the will••
	• The will is not to decide what is good or to be pursued
	• Rather, it is to freely assent to the deliverances of the natural light

â€¢ ••The natural light••
	• A divine, objective framework of truth, meaning, and value
	• Implies that meaning and value flow from a source other than oneself

â€¢ ••Theistic vision••
	• The source of truth and goodness is God
	• This vision is central to Descartes' philosophy

â€¢ ••Freedom of enlightenment••
	• Human freedom is the spontaneous assent to what is rationally perceived as true or good
	• This conception of freedom is "submissive" in the sense that it is a spontaneous outflow of the will

â€¢ ••Submission vs. coercion••
	• The will is not coerced or constrained, but rather freely assents to what is perceived as good or true
	• This is not "submission" in the sense of being reluctantly coerced

â€¢ ••The relationship between freedom and science••
	• Descartes' scientific and technological ambition requires a vision of human life with a meaningful place in the scheme of things
	• This vision is necessary to guide and direct the pursuit of the good and the meaningful

â€¢ ••Objective goodness and meaningfulness••
	• There is objective goodness and meaningfulness that is independently worthy of pursuit
	• This is in contrast to modern ideas that cut ethics free from any substantive vision of the good

â€¢ ••Descartes' rejection of modern ethics••
	• Descartes would not subscribe to modern ideas that make the good and the meaningful "up for grabs"
	• He would reject expressivist and instrumentalist conceptions of ethics that cut ethics free from any substantive vision of the good

â€¢ ••The importance of final causes••
	• Descartes believed that the search for final causes (or purposes) is essential to understanding the natural world
	• This implies that there is a deeper, objective meaning to the world that is worthy of pursuit.

• ••Descartes' views on science and teleology••
	• Descartes saw scientific explanation as working through quantitative laws.
	• He warned against invoking the purposes of the creator, which were "hidden in the inscrutable abyss of his wisdom".
• ••Descartes' views on the cosmos••
	• The cosmos is a divine creation, whose laws of operation bear the stamp of the divine intelligence.
	• Physics may have no room for teleology, but the cosmos is not a meaningless concatenation of blind particle interactions.
• ••Descartes' views on human life••
	• Human beings can orient their lives towards an objective source of meaning and value through the gift of reason.
	• Human beings can achieve fulfilling and meaningful lives despite the operation of their bodies in accordance with the laws of corpuscular interaction.
• ••Descartes' views on the human longing for meaning••
	• The human longing for meaning can be fully satisfied in a world where God is taken out of the equation.
	• This is a question that contemporary philosophy has still not finally settled.
• ••Descartes' views on doubt and certainty••
	• Descartes used "hyperbolical doubt" to question everything, including his own existence.
	• He believed that he could be certain of his own existence as a thinking being.
• ••Descartes' views on the nature of reality••
	• Descartes believed that the world is a quantitative universe, governed by laws of corpuscular interaction.
	• He believed that human beings can still achieve a deep understanding of the world through reason.
• ••Descartes' views on the relationship between body and soul••
	• Descartes believed that the body and soul are separate entities.
	• He believed that the soul is a non-physical substance that interacts with the body.
• ••Descartes' views on the passions and emotions••
	• Descartes believed that the passions and emotions are a natural part of human experience.
	• He believed that the passions and emotions can be understood and managed through reason.
• ••Descartes' views on the nature of knowledge••
	• Descartes believed that knowledge is a product of reason and experience.
	• He believed that knowledge is a matter of understanding the world through clear and distinct ideas.
• ••Descartes' legacy and influence••
	• Descartes' ideas have had a profound influence on Western philosophy.
	• His views on the nature of reality, knowledge, and human experience continue to be debated and discussed today.

	• We can be motivated by empirical circumstances or by principles

â€¢ ••The Importance of Balance••
	• Human action requires a balance between virtue and joy
	• We must find a balance between moral principles and individual inclinations.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Problem of Moral Reflection••
	• The author cannot take each circumstance as an occasion for moral reflection
	• This is because moral reflection requires a continuous train of thought
	• The author cannot start a new train of moral reflection at every moment

â€¢ ••Kant's Answer to the Meaning of Life••
	• The author is pursuing a Kantian answer to the question "What is the meaning of life?"
	• The strategy is to approach it by way of a smaller, more manageable question: "What gives meaning to human action?"
	• The answer is a balance of duty and joy, with no one "right" balance

â€¢ ••The Perplexity of the Meaning of Life••
	• If the author knows what makes each action meaningful, what room is left for the general question of the meaningfulness of human life?
	• The author seems to have all the resources needed to make decisions based on duty or happiness

â€¢ ••Kant's Reply to the Challenge••
	• Kant insists that we cannot view each episode of moral striving in isolation
	• We must view them as a whole, as a unity

â€¢ ••The Activity of Taking a Plurality as a Unity••
	• This activity is unexceptionable and pervasive in ordinary experience
	• Examples include cognizing a straight line or a house as a unity

â€¢ ••The Moral Context••
	• Why can we not rest content with cognizing particular actions as such?
	• This is a key question for Kant, as it relates to his philosophy about legitimate and bogus questions

â€¢ ••Kant's Philosophy on Objects and Perception••
	• In the Critique of Pure Reason, Kant argues that we can cognize objects only as they are given to our senses
	• Questions about how objects may be apart from how they affect us are illegitimate

â€¢ ••The Importance of Considering the Whole••
	• Kant's philosophy emphasizes the importance of considering the whole, rather than individual parts
	• This is relevant to the question of the meaningfulness of human life

â€¢ ••The Challenge of Moral Decision-Making••
	• The author acknowledges that there will be hard cases and considerations of time pressure, fatigue, etc.
	• However, the author knows that what gives an action its meaning is the fact that it draws duty (and so freedom) into play

â€¢ ••The Implications for the Meaning of Life••
	• If the author knows what makes each action meaningful, what implications does this have for the general question of the meaningfulness of human life?
	• Kant's philosophy suggests that we must consider the whole, rather than individual parts, in order to answer this question.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Kant's Critique of Absurd Questions••
	• Kant argues that asking certain questions is absurd and can lead to misleading answers.
	• He uses the example of trying to milk a billy-goat while holding a sieve underneath.

â€¢ ••Transcendental Illusions••
	• Kant identifies "transcendental illusions" as ideas that are formed by taking a plurality as a unity.
	• Examples include the concept of a substantial soul and the world as an absolute totality.

â€¢ ••The Problem of Unifying Pluralities••
	• Kant argues that we tend to unify disparate elements into a bogus object, such as a soul or the world.
	• This unification is illegitimate and leads to absurd conclusions.

â€¢ ••The Meaning of Life••
	• Kant asks whether we can advance from the meaning of individual actions to the meaning of our actions as a whole.
	• He suggests that this advance is not like unifying points into a line, but rather like trying to milk a billy-goat.

â€¢ ••The Legitimacy of Asking about the Meaning of Life••
	• Kant argues that we need an argument to show the legitimacy of asking about the meaning of our actions taken together.
	• He emphasizes the importance of explicating the notion of a "finite rational being".

â€¢ ••The Highest Good••
	• Kant defines the highest good as a world in which happiness is apportioned according to virtue.
	• This concept is central to his Critique of Practical Reason.

â€¢ ••The Categorical Imperative••
	• Kant appeals to the categorical imperative to justify the idea of a highest good.
	• He argues that the moral law commands us to make the highest possible good in a world the final object of all our conduct.

â€¢ ••The Meaning of Life as the Highest Good••
	• Kant suggests that promoting a world in which virtue is rewarded with happiness is itself "the meaning of life".
	• This idea is central to his philosophy, but may be subject to doubt and criticism.

â€¢ ••Doubts about the Categorical Imperative••
	• Some may doubt whether Kant has shown that we are required to find a purpose or goal for all our conduct.
	• This doubt may come from within the Kantian framework, questioning the command of the categorical imperative.

â€¢ ••The Interest of the Highest Good••
	• Kant's concept of the highest good is of undeniable interest, fitting with creatures who feel the pull of both duty and happiness.
	• However, some may doubt whether he has shown that this concept is of more than just natural interest.

Here are 10 bullets summarizing the text with sub-bullets:

• ••Kant's views on happiness and morality••
	• Kant's highest good should appear as objectively necessary, but this might be a transcendental illusion
	• In Religion within the Boundaries of Mere Reason (1793), Kant sets out a different approach to the highest good
• ••The central passage of Religion within the Boundaries of Mere Reason••
	• Morality doesn't need an end for right conduct, but an end proceeds from morality anyway
	• The idea of a highest good arises from morality and is not its foundation
• ••The idea of a highest good••
	• It's the idea of an object that unites duty and happiness
	• It's not an empty idea, but rather meets our natural need for an ultimate end
• ••The relationship between morality and the highest good••
	• Morality presents itself in general and particular terms
	• Particular actions must resemble one another as species of a higher genus
• ••The motivation behind actions••
	• Particular actions require some intended result
	• The general kind of action (morality) also requires an intended result
• ••The goal of morality••
	• The crucial question is, "At what does the larger project of morality aim?"
	• Kant answers that morality aims at a world in which happiness is apportioned according to virtue
• ••The meaning of life••
	• In both the Critique of Practical Reason and Religion within the Boundaries of Mere Reason, the meaning of life is the pursuit of the highest good
	• The difference is that in the later work, the impetus to pursue the highest good comes from a different train of thought
• ••The new train of thought••
	• All actions must have some goal or end
	• Right conduct, as a kind of action, must have some goal or end
	• The goal or end that best fits creatures who answer both to duty and to happiness is a world in which virtue is rewarded with happiness
• ••The emergence of the meaning of life••
	• The meaning of life, in the sense of the pursuit of the highest or complete good, emerges from a generic feature of human action
	• This feature is that all actions must have some goal or end

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Kant's Argument for the Highest Good••
	• The highest good is the ultimate goal of human actions
	• It requires a world where virtue is rewarded with proportional happiness
	• This is not plausible without a supreme cause of nature

â€¢ ••The Need for a Supreme Cause••
	• A supreme cause of nature is necessary for the highest good
	• This cause must be a rational being with a will
	• This being is the author of nature, i.e. God

â€¢ ••The Moral Necessity of Assuming God's Existence••
	• Committing to the highest good requires assuming God's existence
	• This is a moral necessity, not a logical one
	• God is necessary for the possibility of the highest good

â€¢ ••Morality Leads to Religion••
	• Morality inevitably leads to the idea of a mighty moral lawgiver
	• This lawgiver is God, who wills the ultimate end of creation
	• Human morality is extended to the idea of a divine moral law

â€¢ ••The Question of the Ultimate Human End••
	• Shall we extend the meaning of life to include the moral postulate of God's existence?
	• This is a difficult question, and one about which Kant had second thoughts

â€¢ ••Subjective vs. Objective Necessity••
	• Belief in God is subjectively necessary, not objectively necessary
	• It is not necessary to assume God's existence as a ground of all obligation
	• This is because reason itself is the ground of all obligation

â€¢ ••The Arc of Kant's Argument••
	• The argument starts with individual human actions
	• It moves to all human actions taken together
	• It ends with the idea of a supreme cause of nature, i.e. God

â€¢ ••The Categorical Imperative••
	• The categorical imperative is the requirement to treat persons with respect
	• This is the foundation of Kant's ethics
	• It is necessary for answering the question of the meaning of life

â€¢ ••The Question of Whether to Believe in God••
	• Does endorsing the categorical imperative carry us to belief in God?
	• Or does it only carry us to a commitment to the highest good?
	• This is a difficult question, and one that Kant himself struggled with

â€¢ ••The Difficulty of Answering the Question••
	• The question of the meaning of life is difficult to answer
	• It requires a deep understanding of Kant's philosophy
	• It is a question that has puzzled philosophers for centuries

â€¢ ••Kant's views on morality••

â€¢ ••Schopenhauer's approach to life's meaning••
	• Schopenhauer asks whether life is worth living in view of reality's worth
	• He approaches the question from an objective, mind-independent perspective

â€¢ ••Schopenhauer's concept of the "will"••
	• The "will" refers to ultimate reality or the "thing-in-itself"
	• The will is characterized as an aimless, meaningless impulse
	• It is an endless striving with no end in view

â€¢ ••Properties of the will••
	• The will is devoid of knowledge and is a blind, irresistible urge
	• It is absolutely free and entirely self-determining
	• There is no law for the will

â€¢ ••Relationship between the will and the world••
	• The will constitutes the spatio-temporal world
	• The world is a battle-ground of tormented and agonized beings
	• Every living being is a living grave of others

â€¢ ••Conflict in the natural world••
	• The natural world is characterized by conflict and strife
	• This conflict is a revelation of the will's variance with itself
	• The animal kingdom is a prime example of this conflict

â€¢ ••The will's relationship to space and time••
	• The will is beyond the distinction between space and time
	• It is beyond the subject-object distinction
	• It is one beyond the distinction between one and many

â€¢ ••Implications of the will's nature••
	• The will's aimlessness and meaninglessness imply that life is also aimless and meaningless
	• The world is a morally objectionable place due to the will's manifestation as individual beings in conflict

â€¢ ••Schopenhauer's view of life••
	• Life is a series of torturing deaths and self-maintenance
	• Every living being is a predator and a prey
	• The world is a place of perpetual conflict and strife

â€¢ ••Conditional meaning of life••
	• Life may have a conditional meaning to one or more people
	• This meaning will dissolve when they die
	• The meaning of life may be subjective and personal.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Schopenhauer's View of the World••
	• He believes the world is essentially meaningless and fundamentally vicious
	• Reality is driven by a senseless impulse called "Will"
	• This Will manifests as a world where sentient beings sustain themselves by devouring each other

â€¢ ••The Nature of Will••
	• Will is a cannibalistic and morally repugnant entity
	• It drives individuals to be selfish and animalistic
	• The world would be better off without it

â€¢ ••Schopenhauer's Critique of Humanity••
	• He believes people are contemptible and their fate is one of want, wretchedness, and death
	• This is a result of their inherent selfishness and animalistic nature

â€¢ ••The Pessimistic Tone of Schopenhauer's Philosophy••
	• His philosophy is often seen as pessimistic and misanthropic
	• He is criticized for his negative views on humanity and the world

â€¢ ••A Profound Meaning in Life••
	• Despite his pessimistic views, Schopenhauer believes there is a profound meaning in life
	• This meaning is only accessible to those who have transcended their desires and achieved a state of will-less awareness

â€¢ ••The Ascetic's Awareness••
	• Schopenhauer identifies an exceptional state of consciousness that is will-less, peaceful, and clear-minded
	• This state is achieved through the minimization of desire and is associated with transcendence and supreme satisfaction

â€¢ ••The Transcendent Perspective••
	• From the ascetic's perspective, the spatio-temporal world appears as "nothing"
	• This perspective is associated with a meaningful experience that renders the ordinary world unimportant

â€¢ ••The Relationship Between Will and Transcendence••
	• Schopenhauer believes that the Will is the driving force behind the world's suffering and selfishness
	• Transcendence is achieved by denying and turning away from the Will

â€¢ ••The Role of Philosophy••
	• Schopenhauer believes that philosophy can only express negative knowledge
	• However, he also suggests that a positive knowledge of transcendence can be acquired through the ascetic's awareness

â€¢ ••The Significance of Schopenhauer's Philosophy••
	• His philosophy is significant because it offers a profound and meaningful perspective on life
	• Despite its pessimistic tone, it provides a way to transcend the suffering and selfishness of the world.

â€¢ ••Schopenhauer's Denial of the Will••
	• Schopenhauer's philosophy is based on the denial of the will (asceticism)
	• This denial leads to a state of ecstasy, rapture, or union with God
	• However, this state cannot be called knowledge, as it lacks a subject-object form

â€¢ ••Parallels with Hegel's Philosophy••
	• Despite their differences, Schopenhauer's philosophy parallels Hegel's in its emphasis on self-consciousness
	• Both philosophers believe that reality becomes aware of itself through human beings
	• However, their views on ultimate reality differ: Hegel's is rational, while Schopenhauer's is non-rational

â€¢ ••Schopenhauer's View of Will••

	• Despite its aimlessness, Will becomes aware of itself through human beings
	• This awareness is achieved through philosophical reflection

â€¢ ••The Purpose of Life••

â€¢ ••The Nature of Reality••

	• This realization is a turning point, leading to a rejection of the will and a turn towards asceticism

â€¢ ••The Role of Human Beings••

	• Self-knowledge reveals the suffering and impermanence of the world
	• This knowledge motivates a turn away from the will and towards asceticism
	• It leads to a rejection of egoism and the principium individuationis

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Will and Human Beings••
	• Will is a non-rational being that becomes aware of itself through human beings
	• Human beings are manifestations of Will that come to know themselves through philosophical reflection
	• This relationship is central to Schopenhauer's philosophy

â€¢ ••The Significance of Asceticism••

â€¢ ••The Limits of Knowledge••
	• Schopenhauer's philosophy emphasizes the limits of knowledge
	• True knowledge is only accessible through personal experience and philosophical reflection
